PREFACE 

Not a few new words have come into the language of recent years. Some old words, little used in past years, have come into general use. The latest edition of Webster's international makes use of some new diacritical characters, and makes some changes in pronunciation and definition that should be noted. These are the technical reasons for the appearance of a new business speller. 

The technical reasons above enumerated are alone sufficient to justify the bringing out of a new book. There are other reasons, however, that have made the New Business Speller a practical necessity. Commercial education has developed. Courses are more advanced than they were, and students are of a higher grade. 

Business men are yearly becoming more critical. 

The commercial education wave has brought the teaching of spelling into the high schools and academies. The whole field of business education is broader, and more is required in spelling, as well as in the other branches.

For these reasons the tendency has been to increase the number of words in the spelling books. New Business Speller has 6,000 words, or about 1,000 words more than any other. So carefully have these words been selected, however, that every word in the book will be seen to be a useful one. The repetition of words has been worked out in such painstaking detail that, while all needed repetition for review has been given, the book is not bulky, nor is there any wasteful, useless repetition.

The day of the "word list" speller has passed. The unscientific compilations of miscellaneous words that in times past have paraded under the name of speller cannot be expected to last. The subject of spelling can be taught as scientifically, with the same high regard for completeness, systematic development, and effective treatment, as any other school subject. The aim of the New Business Speller is to give such a treatment. 

Note the following features: 

1. There are 200 lessons. This provides for forty weeks of ive days each. 

2. Many new words. Instances are Lesson 122, automobile terms, and Lesson 124, aeroplane terms. 

3. The total number of words is 6,000. This includes all words in daily use and many words that are important but are only occasionally used. 

4. In the selection of words special attention has been paid to the needs of high school students. iii

5. Teachers of stenography will find the classifications easily adaptable to the requirements of their classes. Instances are 

Lessons 61 to 64, inclusive, and other lessons on prefixes, suffixes, derivatives, root words, compound words, and the topical classifications of words throughout. 

6. The monotonous and space-wasting plan of arranging all lessons alike, and showing definitions of all words, has not been followed. Pronunciation is given when necessary. Definitions are given where necessary. The author has omitted either pronunciation or definition, or both, when it has seemed desirable. 

This plan reduces the study of spelling to its lowest terms, saves space, and provides variety. 66 7. Frequently the use of a word in a sentence or phrase is of more assistance to the student than a formal definition. The lists of common expressions" which are given in connection with the lessons constitute a welcome relief from the monotony of constant definition. When definitions are given, they are given in more complete form than in most spellers. 

8. Spelling is developed scientifically. The rules for pronunciation, accent, syllabication, spelling, compounding of words, and the study of word building are made the basis of the work for the first seventy-two lessons. While the spelling itself is constantly emphasized as the important thing, and learning to spell as the ultimate object, the pupil feels that his daily work is a part of a broad educational scheme, and that spelling is a subject to be studied, not merely a daily grind of memorization. Beyond Lesson 

72 there is classification of words by topic, words common in one subject, such as history or geography, being grouped together for psychological reasons. Incidentally, such grouping permits economies in the definitions: thus, twenty-five kitchen utensils can be listed under a heading, without definition. 

9. Following page 190 is an index which includes all words of the speller, alphabetically arranged. Each word is followed by a page reference which shows where it first appears. In the index, bold face type is used to indicate words that have been pronounced and defined; italic indicates words for which the pronunciation is shown, but not the definition. 

10. Special attention has been given to words that are hard to spell. See Lessons 57, 71, and others, and note the repetition of the more difficult words in the reviews. 

11. Every fifth lesson is a review. When spelling is taught every day, the review lessons will come on the same day every week. These reviews contain fifty words instead of twenty-five. 

Every twentieth lesson is a general review which contains one hundred words and should be used as a "spelling down" list. 

If oral "spelling down" lessons are objected to, a "writing down" lesson can be conducted as follows: Dictate all the words. Then require the class to stand while correct spellings are read aloud, iv

each student taking his seat as soon as a word is spelled which he has written wrong. Those standing when all words have been read are the winners. 

12. The principle of visualization has been utilized. Every word is presented to the eye first, just as the eye must be accustomed to see it, unmutilated by diacritical marks. Bold face type, somewhat larger than the rest of the book, is used for the first column, in which the words first appear. Respelling phonetically to show pronunciation is a violation of the principle of visualization. However proper such phonetic respelling might be in a dictionary, it is unquestionably improper in a speller. The New Business Speller indicates pronunciation entirely by diacritical markings, without phonetic misspelling, except in case of a very few words. 

Particular attention is called to the care with which the detail of the book has been worked out. Examine each lesson carefully. 

Innumerable excellencies will present themselves-minor points, not worthy of enumeration in a list of important features but in the aggregate making the strongest feature of this or any speller. 

These innumerable minor essentials of perfection, these constant evidences of care, can be relied upon as sure indications that the book excels in completeness, consistency, accuracy, and pedagogical development.

During the last few years the teaching of spelling has been studied scientifically as never before. The entire matter of tests and measurements has come forward and great effort has been put forth to discover what words children in the schools should learn to spell, and how. Spelling scales from which tests may be taken have been devised and methods of procedure worked out for utilizing them. So the New Business Speller has been supplemented; to it have been added and explained five new groups of words representing all the new spelling interests: 

1. Two Hundred New Words from Webster: In Webster's New 

International Dictionary (1923) are listed several hundred new words. 

Of these 200 of the most important are given, pronounced and defined as there indicated. 

2. Two Hundred Words from Thorndike, carefully chosen from his celebrated list of 10,000, found by thorough investigation to be the most commonly used words of the language and embodied in his 

The Teacher's Word Book. 

3. One Hundred Words from Ayres' well-known spelling scale of the 1,000 most commonly used words. 

4. One Hundred Words from Buckingham's addition of 505 to the

Ayres' scale. 

5. The One Hundred "Demons" found by Jones to be the words most commonly misspelled.

With these additions and these explanations, the publishers are confident that the New Business Speller will continue to meet the needs of a constantly growing clientele. 

Chicago, Illinois, January, 1924

ORTHOEPY Correct Pronunciation an Aid in Spelling 

The spelling of the words of a purely phonetic language, if any such existed, would be simply a matter of learning to recognize and name the letters, to know their sound values, and to pronounce correctly. But there is no language in which all of the words are spelled exactly as they are pronounced. In proportion as a language is unphonetic do the difficulties of its orthography increase. 

In a language as unphonetic as our own, where the sound ē, for instance, may be represented by e (me), ei (seize), ie (siege) ea (read), ¿ (machine), eo (people), ee (need), ae (aerie), correct pronunciation is by no means an infallible guide to correct spelling. 

It is, however, true that even in our unphonetic tongue many words are spelled as they are pronounced, and therefore one who pronounces clearly and accurately is more likely to spell correctly than one who is slovenly and inaccurate in utterance. 

Standard of Pronunciation 

The editors of a dictionary do not, as some people think, create the standard of pronunciation. They merely reduce to written form the usage of the best speakers. One might think that there would be found great diversity of pronunciation even among educated people, and there is diversity, but there is also a surprising agreement in the words of the ordinary vocabulary. There are certain local divergences, certain pronunciations peculiar to certain localities, but these are largely ignored by the editors of a dictionary. In the case of words having more than one pronunciation in good use, both are given.

Any standard dictionary may safely be used as a guide in pronunciation or spelling, but as Webster's International is in general use in the schools, this speller follows the system of diacritical markings in the latest edition of this dictionary---the 1923 edition. 

Some slight changes from former editions are to be noted. This speller does not indicate pronunciation by respelling words phonetically, however, as does Webster, but adheres to the proper spelling, marking the letters whenever necessary to indicate the sounds. 

Only in rare unavoidable cases has respelling phonetically been resorted to, and then only in brackets following the correct spelling.

Cognates 

Cognates are pairs of sounds produced by the organs of speech in the same or nearly the same position. One of the pair has tone (subvocal); the other is a mere breathing (aspirate). 

As children of foreign parentage are very likely to substitute one of the pair of cognates for the other, especially an aspirate for its corresponding subvocal, careful attention should be given to drill upon the following table. Aspirate pin 

Subvocal ball tin done kill go ch chin (g) jam th thin th fun this verb 

X (gz) exact buzz z(h) azure x (ks) box sh see shine

IX

Common Errors

Besides the above noted substitution of aspirates for subvocals, children of foreign parentage commonly substitute w for initial v (woice for voice), d for th (fader for father), y for j (yump for jump), t for th (tin for thin).

Another very common error is to omit the initial sound of ŉ in wh (wen for when). It should be noted that in pronouncing wh the order of the utterance of the sounds is inverted, h coming first.

All children (and adults) are more or less slovenly in the pronunciation of words ending in two or more consonants (singin' for singing, col' for cold, las' for last), especially when the next word begins with a consonant. 

All these errors in pronunciation are likely to results in errors in spelling, and should, therefore, be made the subject of special drill.

List for pronouncing drill on cognates: bridge then jump very measure please easy that John people George 

this thick never jolly white bring mother where thumb which when there Pronunciation In a monosyllable or accented syllable consisting of or ending in a vowel, the vowel usually has its long sound:-na'tion, valu a'tion.

In a monosyllable or accented syllable ending in silent e, the sounded vowel usually has its long sound:-mine, di vine'. In an unaccented syllable ending in silent e the sounded vowel usually has its modified long sound:-sen'âte. 

C, s, or t, when preceded by an accented syllable and followed by e or i before another vowel, usually takes the sound of sh:ocean, gracious, mission, nutritious. It has not been considered necessary in this speller to respell to show this sound. 

C and g when followed by e, i, or y are usually sounded like s and j:-mercy, city, cent, George, ginger, gymnast.

Syllabication 

A syllable is a vowel or vowels alone or in combination with a consonant or consonants uttered with one impulse of the voice. 

It should be noted that a vowel is the nucleus of a syllable, except in the case of l, m, and n, which may constitute such nucleus: -ca ble, pris m, tak en.

Syllabication is the separation of words into syllables. It is an aid in oral spelling as it helps to give a clearer mental picture of a word, and it is an essential in connected writing and in typewriting, in order to divide correctly words which run over to a succeeding line. The syllabication shown in the pronunciation column in this book is for pronunciation only, and does not necessarily indicate the correct division of the word. 

General Rules for Syllabication 

A consonant between two vowels is usually joined to the second unless the first vowel is short: ra ging, to ken, hab it. 

Exception. x should not begin a syllable:—ex act.

Two consonants between two vowels are usually separated unless the first vowel is long or the following vowel is in an accented syllable:-bar ley, car ry, bu gler, o blige. 

Exception. See above:-anx ious.

Two vowels coming together and sounded separately belong to separate syllables:-vac u um. 

Exercise. Write the following paragraph in several ways so as to bring the different words of more than one syllable at the ends of the lines:

There are two general rules to be followed in dividing a word into syllables: First, the word should be so divided as to give ease and smoothness of utterance; and second, it should be se divided as to respect the origin, or derivation, of the word. Accent

Accent is a particular stress of the voice upon one or more syllables of a word:-ab do' men, trans' por ta' tion.

Where there are two accents in a word, the stronger (primary) is usually near the end of the word; the weaker (secondary), near the beginning:-su' per in tend' ent.

In some words of two syllables a change in the part of speech is shown by a change in accent:-ab' sent (adj.), ab sent' (verb). 

Accent is important in spelling only in that it is necessary to correct pronunciation. Definitions 

A monosyllable is a word of one syllable:-rub.

A polysyllable is a word of more than one syllable:-ordinary. 

A primitive word (also called radical or root word) is one not derived from another word by the addition of a prefix or suffix:-rob. XI

A prefix is a syllable or syllables placed at the beginning of a word to modify its meaning:-unhappy.

A suffix is a syllable or syllables placed at the end of a word to modify its meaning:-happiness.

A derivative is a word derived from a primitive word by the addition of a prefix or suffix or both:-robber, unhappiness. 

A diphthong is a union of two vowel sounds in one syllable:-boil. 

A digraph is two signs or characters combined to express a single sound:-head, bath.

A trigraph is three letters united so as to have but one sound:adieu.

Rules for Spelling 

There are exceptions to every rule for spelling, but the following rules are sufficiently general in their application to be worth remembering.

RULE 1. The letters f, l, and s at the end of monosyllables and immediately following single vowels are generally doubled:cliff, toll, mass.

This rule also applies to ƒ and s (but not l) in polysyllables:sheriff, express, canal.

RULE 2. A consonant after a diphthong or a double vowel is rarely doubled:-moan, moon.

RULE 3. In derivatives from words ending in c, the letter k is inserted after c:-panicky.

RULE 4. Monosyllables and words accented on the last syllable ending in a single consonant preceded by a single vowel or a vowel after qu double the final consonant on the addition of a suffix beginning with a vowel:-rob, robbing; compel, compelled; quit, quitted.

RULE 5. The final consonant is not doubled when a suffix beginning with a vowel is added, when (a) The final consonant is preceded by a diphthong or by a vowel digraph:-daub, dauber. (b) The accent of a word ending in a single consonant falls on any other syllable than the last:-revel, reveling. (c) The word ends in two different consonants:-perform, performer. (d) The derivative does not retain the accent of the root:prefer, preference.

RULE 6. Finale of a primitive word is dropped on the addition of a suffix beginning with a vowel:-come, coming.

XII

RULE 7. Finale of a primitive word is usually retained on the addition of a suffix beginning with a consonant:-care, careful. 

RULE 8. Final y when preceded by a consonant is changed to i on the addition of a suffix:-happy, happier. 

RULE 9. Final y when preceded by a vowel remains unchanged on the addition of a suffix:-boy, boyish.

RULE 10. Nouns ending in ƒ or fe usually form their plurals by changing the ƒ to v and adding es or s:-calf, calves; knife, knives. 

RULE 11. Nouns ending in o preceded by a vowel form their plurals regularly by adding s:-cameo, cameos.

NOTE. The spelling of the plurals of nouns ending in o preceded by a consonant is not uniform. These words must be looked up. 

Compound Words 

A compound word is one made up of two or more simple words. 

It may or may not be written with the hyphen: -son-in-law, blackboard.

No rules can be given which will determine all cases in which the compound word is hyphenated. In general the hyphen is used, although the tendency is toward its omission in some words formerly containing it. For instance, today, tonight, and tomorrow are becoming quite common, and blackboard, schoolhouse, and classroom are sanctioned. 

The following rules will guide in the use of the hyphen: 

Use the hyphen

RULE 12. When ex precedes titles:-ex-president. 

RULE 13. When a numeral adjective precedes rate or hand:

second-rate.

RULE 14. When adjective expressions are compounded:

light-haired. 

RULE 15. In numbers below one hundred made by adding two numbers:-twenty-five.

RULE 16. Between the numerator and denominator of frac tions:-two-thirds.

RULE 17. In the denominator of a fraction when made up of two words:-ten-thousandths.

Whether an expression is a group of independent words or a compound word, is a matter of fact not covered by any general rule. Custom governs in each case, and custom is not consistent. 

When in doubt, the dictionary should be consulted-if the word is usually used as a compound word it will be found there. XIII

NOTES 

Words are respelled for pronunciation in this book only when it is impossible to show the correct pronunciation by diacritical marks.

Silent letters are printed in italics, thus:-height. 

The vowels in the accented syllables are marked for pronunciation, but those in the unaccented syllables are not usually marked. 

Consonants in all syllables are marked when necessary. XVI

NOTE. The above definitions are literal, as taken from the exact meanings of the roots, prefixes, and suffixes used. Many derivatives, however, have acquired a significance that requires a less literal definition. Thus, postpone means to place later; mistake means to take or have in mind wrongly; prefer means to set before or above (something else) in liking; preclude means to shut out or prevent; induce means to lead on or persuade; induction, a process of reasoning from particular to general truths; preference, the setting of one thing before another in liking.

LESSON 45 

Review

The man whom you saw gambling is a knave, for he stole an auger and a bridle from the barn, then went to a neighboring church and took two silver vases from the altar. He avoided arrest by jumping into a boat moored on the beach and rowing to a steamer lying at anchor. Later he was arrested and indicted by the grand jury. Through the efforts of his counsel he was released on bail and immediately boarded a steamer to go abroad; but failing to secure a berth, he altered his course and took the next train for the capital. 

He mingled with the horde of sightseers who were viewing the capitol and again increased his hoard of ill-gotten wealth by stealing a fourteen-carat gold watch from a man who was one of a bridal party viewing the sights. Finding that he did not have a cent in his pockets he left the crowd, and went to a second-hand shop where he sold the watch. An hour later he was arrested, but was released on bail. When the time of his trial was near, he found that his case was fourth on the court calendar. He returned for trial, although he had great fear that he would be convicted and sent to prison. lessen lesson levee levy loan lone mantel mantle mean mien

LESSON 51 

Review

As there had been a prophecy of a hot summer, the principal of a school planned to spend his summer vacation scaling mountain peaks. A further inducement to travel was found in the fact that his profits from investments the past year had been greatly in excess of those of former years. Acting on the advice of a friend, he bought two new suits, one of serge, the other of cassimere. 

These with what he had he thought would serve him in all straits. 

Then, too, he found it impossible to carry much baggage. He <ried by many ingenious arguments to induce his friend to accompany him, but his urging had no effect. So he started out alone to cross the plains. He had bought stationery, and being little affected by the noise and jar of the moving train, began to write a letter to his mother. He told her of all the little details of his journey-how the plains in places seemed like a great desert, what immense rabbits he saw from the car window, how he had been advised by his friend to overcome possible mountain sickness, what he should wear when climbing, what he had had for dessert on the diner, how he expected to go down in a mine, not to look for a rich vein, but just to see the metal in its native state, and how he had thought out an ingenious scheme for better smelting of ores, which scheme he purposed proposing to a certain practical mine owner. He said the principle upon which it was based was so simple that he was sure he could guarantee success at the outset. Only slight changes in the present method need be made to bring about effects truly marvelous. He also wrote her about a deceased friend of whom he had been very fond. This friend had owned a piece of land in one of the mining districts which he had prophesied would show a rich vein of silver. He himself expected to go there on this trip and, if there was easy access for a railroad, to buy the strip. He might have written much more, but there was a call for supper from the porter who passed by his seat, so he brought his letter to a close.

LESSON 172 Synonyms 

The lessons from 172 to 184 inclusive are largely reviews and also furnish drill in synonyms. The horizontal lines are made up of synonymous words, but it should be borne in mind that no two words are exact synonyms. abandon relinquish resign surrender abasement degradation disgrace humiliation abbreviate shorten curtail retrench abdicate renounce vacate forego abettor accessory (noun) accomplice confederate abhor detest loathe abominate abide continue remain tarry abiding continuing unchangeable permanent ability efficiency talent aptitude abjure forswear disown repudiate abnormal unusual anomalous irregular abode residence domicile habitation abolish nullify State the difference in meaning between abandon and surrender abbreviate and retrench continuing and unchangeable abasement and humiliation abide and tarry ability and talent accomplice and confederate abnormal and anomalous Common 

Expressions abnormal growth nullify the conditions relinquish the claim resign his position curtail expenses accomplice in crime abdicate the throne a disgrace to the profession to abjure allegiance abolish slavery continue to increase an abiding love a permanent position an aptitude for management repudiate the claim an unusual occurrence strive for efficiency vacate the premises

LESSON 173 Synonyms aboriginal native primitive original abortive unsuccessful fruitless failing abrupt precipitous unceremonious brusque absolute unlimited unconditional peremptory absolve exonerate discharge acquit abstinence forbearance self-restraint moderation abstract summary abridgment digest abundance plenty affluence luxuriance abusive scurrilous opprobrious insulting accede consent concur acquiesce accelerate expedite dispatch hasten accident 

disaster catastrophe accountable responsible peremptory pěr❜emp to ry 

Notice the accent and the sound of the first e. *Notice that there are only four syllables in this word. 

Distinguish between aboriginal and original absolute and peremptory absolve and discharge abstinence and moderation accede and concur absolute control expedite matters accelerate and dispatch consent and acquiesce abstract and summary affluence and plenty accident and disaster

Common Expressions an abrupt or precipitous descent a brusque or unceremonious manner an abortive attempt failing health responsible for the omission primitive customs aboriginal or native inhabitants unconditional surrender a fruitless effort exonerate or absolve from blame total abstinence abstract of the deed summary of the argument digest of the news luxuriance of vegetation lived in affluence blamed for the casualty scurrilous language

LESSON 174 Synonyms accuracy precision exactness correctness accusation arraignment impeachment indictment acknowledgment recognition admission avowal advantageous profitable useful beneficial antipathy hatred repugnance dislike apparel garments raiment attire barbarous uncivilized merciless brutal barrier obstacle hindrance obstruction blasphemous irreverent profane sacrilegious candid impartial unbiased ingenuous champion protector defender advocate circumspect cautious prudent discreet boundary limit

Antipathy means a feeling against; sympathy means a feeling for; hence the two words are opposites.

Circumspect means, literally, looking about or around, hence, watchful on every side. 

Which is the stronger word in each of the following cases? blasphemous or irreverent prudent or discreet hatred or dislike 

Distinguish between advantageous and useful candid and impartial boundary and limit protector and advocate

Common jury brought an indictment profane language unbiased opinion a barbarous or uncivilized people merciless or brutal conduct hindrance to success admission or avowal accusation or arraignment hindrance or obstruction impeachment and indictment profitable and useful barbarous and brutal cautious and discreet

Expressions feeling of antipathy arraignment of the prisoner spoke with precision remove all obstacles an impartial jury in gay attire

NOTE. N (small capital) indicates the nasal tone of the preceding vowel, made by sending out the breath through both nose and mouth.

The n is not sounded-it merely indicates nasalization.
